He’s been spending all his time on this new landscaping project in the backyard, which is a whole other headache with the contractors leaving mud everywhere... \n\nBut anyway, I'm here because since my cousin died, that discipline is just gone. I just lie awake in the dark replaying the night. And then if I do manage to finally drift off, I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning from these... scraps of hospital images. That hospital had this awful, buzzing fluorescent lighting, you know the kind? It gives you a headache just standing in the hallway, and the parking garage was an absolute nightmare to navigate. \n\nBut now I'm going into my executive board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, and with the end of the quarter coming up, I just can't afford to be this off my game.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe more about the thoughts or memories that keep coming back to you when you're trying to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's not just when I'm trying to sleep, actually. It's at the most random, odd moments. Like, if I hear a phone ring late at night, or even just seeing the glare of headlights when I'm driving home on the highway... I get this incredibly sharp picture of that hospital corridor pushing into my mind. Or I'll hear the police officer's voice. I actually had to pull over on I-95 the other day because of it, which made me late for a dinner reservation with my sister. She's always complaining that I prioritize work over family, so being late just gave her more ammunition. We were supposed to go to this new Italian place downtown—the one everyone's been raving about with the homemade pasta—but by the time I got there, they'd given our table away. The hostess was incredibly rude about it, too. You'd think with the prices they charge, they'd have better customer service. \n\nBut yes, when those images or the sound of that officer's voice just force their way into my head, I really have to stop and pause just to steady myself. It's so distracting. And like I said, I just don't have time for distractions right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities because they remind you of the events surrounding your cousin's death?\n\nPatient: Avoid? No, not really. I mean, I can't exactly afford to just stop going places or doing my job. Though, to be completely honest, I *have* been avoiding the country club lately, but that has absolutely nothing to do with... well, with the hospital or my cousin. It's because Richard—my VP of Operations—practically lives on the back nine on Thursdays, and if he corners me one more time about restructuring the IT department, I think I'll lose my mind. \n\nHe’s been pushing for this new cloud integration that is just absurdly expensive. We tried a similar vendor about five years ago and it was a complete disaster. Half our client data got duplicated, and my assistant, Sarah—who is usually wonderful, she actually just had her first baby, a little boy, so cute—she spent three solid weeks manually deleting duplicate files. It was an absolute administrative nightmare, and I refuse to go through it again just because Richard read an article in Forbes. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding things about the death. No, I haven't noticed myself doing that. Like I said, I have a company to run, so I just have to keep moving forward. I don't really have the luxury of hiding out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually negative, blaming yourself, or having trouble experiencing positive emotions since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Negative? Blaming myself? No, I wouldn't say that. The accident was what it was, there was nothing I could have done. But... positive emotions? That's... well, it's been strange. \n\nWe had these prayer gatherings recently for my cousin. My sister insisted on them, of course. She brought over this massive catered spread from that same Italian place, actually—the one that gave away my table. Apparently, they do catering now, though I don't know how they manage it when they can't even manage a simple reservation system. The stuffed mushrooms were completely cold by the time they arrived, and the delivery driver parked right on my husband's new sod. You should have seen my husband's face. He spent three weekends getting that grass perfectly leveled, and this kid in a beat-up Civic just rolls right over it. \n\nBut, um... at the gathering, my nieces were there. They're sweet girls, both in college now—one is at NYU studying finance, which I keep telling her is a brutal industry for women, but she won't listen. They came up and hugged me, trying to comfort me, and I... I did all the right things. I hugged them back, I said the right words, I behaved perfectly properly. But inside? I just felt... nothing. No warmth, no relief, no connection. It was like the affectionate part of my brain just shut off or went quiet. Which is just so frustrating, because normally I love seeing the girls. But I just stood there feeling completely hollow while they cried on my shoulder. And then I had to go deal with the caterer about the cold mushrooms, which took another forty-five minutes on the phone.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin’s death, have you noticed a lasting sense of emptiness, sadness, or lack of interest in things that usually bring you joy—like seeing your nieces or enjoying a good meal?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you mention that, because I was just thinking about those prayer gatherings again. Like I said before, when my nieces hugged me, I did all the right things. I hugged them back, I said the right words... but inside, I just felt absolutely nothing. No warmth, no relief. It was like the affectionate part of me just went completely quiet. \n\nAnd as for meals, honestly, it's hard to enjoy a good meal when the service is so abysmal these days. My husband and I tried to go to our favorite steakhouse last Friday—the one over on West End, where they usually know us and have our table ready. We've been going there for our anniversary for fifteen years. They have this incredible dry-aged ribeye, and the sommelier, Jean-Luc, always remembers that I prefer a full-bodied Cabernet over a Pinot Noir. But this time? Jean-Luc was on vacation in Provence, apparently, and the new guy they had filling in tried to push some trendy natural wine on us. It tasted like kombucha that had gone bad. I told the manager, who is also new, by the way—the old manager, David, retired and moved to Florida to open a bed and breakfast, which sounds exhausting to me, but to each their own. Anyway, the new manager barely apologized. So, between the terrible wine and the lack of service, no, I haven't really been enjoying my meals lately. \n\nBut... oh, yes, the emptiness. Like with the girls at the gathering. I just felt so hollow, which isn't like me at all. I usually love spending time with them, even if I do have to listen to the youngest talk about her finance classes.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin’s death, have you felt a lasting sense of sadness or a loss of pleasure in activities or people that usually bring you joy?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I just said this, didn't I? It's exactly what happened with my nieces at the prayer gatherings. They came up to comfort me, and I did all the proper things—I hugged them back, I said the right things—but I just felt nothing. No warmth, no relief. It was like that affectionate part of me had just gone completely quiet. \n\nWhich, honestly, is how I feel about most things right now. Even my book club. I've been in this book club for twelve years. We're supposed to be reading that new historical fiction novel—the one set in WWII Paris that everyone is raving about. It's on all the bestseller lists. But the woman hosting this month, Linda, she always insists on making these overly complicated appetizers that take away from the actual discussion. Last time she hosted, she made these little endive boats with goat cheese and candied pecans, and I swear, we spent forty-five minutes just talking about where she sourced the pecans. Apparently, there's some artisanal nut shop downtown that charges an absolute fortune. I mean, I appreciate good food, obviously, but we are there to discuss literature, not Linda's grocery shopping habits. \n\nAnd her husband—he always hovers around the kitchen while we're trying to talk. He's an architect, and he just loves to interject his opinions on the structural integrity of the buildings described in the books. It's exhausting. I haven't even opened the book for this month, to be perfectly honest. I just look at it sitting on my nightstand and feel... well, just that same quiet, empty feeling. I just don't have the energy for Linda's endive boats right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to care about or look forward to, like your book club or anything else you normally enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I... well, I thought I just answered that, didn't I? The book club. I haven't even cracked the spine on that Paris book. And normally, I'm the first one finished. I usually have my notes color-coded with those little sticky tabs—the pastel ones, not the neon ones, the neon ones are just too aggressive on the eyes when you're trying to read in bed. My husband hates when I read in bed anyway because the lamp bothers him, even though I bought one of those special directional reading lights that's supposed to only shine on the page. It was eighty dollars at that specialty lighting store on 4th Street, the one that's always having a \"going out of business\" sale but never actually closes. It's ridiculous. \n\nBut yes, no, I haven't been doing my usual things. I just look at the book, or think about going to my Tuesday morning pilates class, and I just... I don't care. I don't want to go. Which reminds me, I really need to cancel my pilates membership if I'm not going, because they charge a fortune and the instructor, Sarah, always plays this awful electronic dance music that just gives me a migraine. But no, I haven't really had any interest in my usual activities.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, such as your book club, pilates, or anything else?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we are going in circles a bit here. Are you getting all this down? Because it's just like I've been saying—it's not just the hobbies. It’s exactly what happened during those prayer gatherings. When my nieces were trying to comfort me, I was behaving properly, doing exactly what you're supposed to do, but I felt absolutely no warmth or relief inside. It really is as if the affectionate part of me has just gone completely quiet. \n\nWhich is honestly so strange for me, especially with my oldest niece, Clara. She just got promoted to regional director at her logistics firm, and normally I would be over the moon for her. I practically mentored her when she was getting her business degree. I always told her she had to project absolute authority in those board meetings, otherwise those older male executives would just talk right over her. And she actually listened to me. She bought this stunning tailored navy pantsuit for her big presentation—Armani, I think, or maybe Hugo Boss, though Boss tends to cut their jackets a little too boxy in the shoulders for my taste. I always have my tailor take them in. He's this wonderful older Italian gentleman on 5th Avenue, though he's talking about retiring soon, which is going to be an absolute disaster for my wardrobe. \n\nAnyway, she looked incredibly sharp. But when she hugged me at the gathering and told me about the promotion, I just... I smiled and said all the right words, but I didn't actually feel happy for her. I didn't feel much of anything. Just that same quiet blankness.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to care about or look forward to, like your book club or anything else you normally enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I really... I mean, I don't know how many different ways I can say the exact same thing. Are you sure you're writing this down correctly? Because I've said it three times now. Yes, I have lost interest in my book club. Yes, I have lost interest in pilates. I don't have the energy for any of it. \n\nI just... I sit at home and I look at the walls. Which, actually, reminds me that I need to call the painters back because the eggshell finish they used in the dining room is completely wrong. I specifically asked for a matte finish, because eggshell just reflects too much light when we have the chandelier on, and it completely ruins the ambiance of dinner parties. Not that I'm hosting any dinner parties right now, obviously. I can barely bring myself to make a cup of tea, let alone plan a menu for twelve people. But still, when you pay that much for professional painters, you expect them to follow simple instructions. The foreman, Dave, he seemed so competent during the consultation, but clearly he doesn't supervise his crew properly. \n\nBut to answer your question again, yes. I don't care about the book club, I don't care about pilates, I don't care about any of the things I usually do. I'm just... I'm not doing them.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you've felt this way—this lack of interest in things you used to enjoy and feeling emotionally numb?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been about three weeks. Ever since the night my cousin died in that awful highway collision. Which, honestly, is another thing entirely, because the state of the highways in this county is just deplorable. I've written to the city council about it at least four times. The merging lane on that specific stretch of I-95 is far too short, and they haven't repainted the reflectors since, what, 2018? I know this because my company's logistics trucks use that route, and my drivers are always complaining about the visibility at night. I even brought it up at the last Chamber of Commerce dinner. The mayor was there, eating this dreadfully dry chicken piccata—you'd think for two hundred dollars a plate they could manage a decent sauce, but it was just rubbery. Anyway, I cornered him near the coat check and told him it was a massive liability. He just gave me that politician smile and nodded. But yes, it's been three weeks since the accident, and I've felt this way ever since. Just... blank. And tired.\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin’s death, have you experienced any changes in your appetite, feelings of guilt, or noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any of that. My appetite is fine, though I suppose I haven't been eating much of the catering we usually get at the office. We switched to this new organic deli downtown, and honestly, it's been a disaster. They promise all these locally sourced, farm-to-table ingredients, but half the time the avocado on the wraps is completely brown by the time it gets to the boardroom. I had my assistant, Michael, call them to complain, and the manager had the audacity to tell him that's just what happens when you don't use preservatives. Which, I'm sorry, I've been making avocado toast at home for years, and a simple squeeze of lemon juice prevents oxidation perfectly well. It's not rocket science, it's basic culinary chemistry. I told Michael to start looking into other vendors, maybe that Italian place on 4th Street. They do a lovely antipasto spread, though they can be a bit heavy-handed with the balsamic glaze. \n\nBut no, I'm not eating slower or moving slower or anything like that. And guilt? No, I don't feel guilty. I just feel... empty.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt more irritable, sad, or emotionally down most of the day, nearly every day, since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I have been more irritable. Sad? I don't know, just... irritable, yes. I actually snapped at my leadership team just yesterday over the most ridiculous things. Small delays in the quarterly projections, and someone used the wrong wording in a client email. I used a tone with them that I would normally consider completely unacceptable for a professional environment. And then I felt so embarrassed afterward, because they hadn't really done anything wrong, they were just doing their jobs. \n\nWhich, speaking of emails, the new IT system they rolled out last month is an absolute nightmare. They promised us this seamless integration with our cloud storage, but half the time, the attachments just disappear into the ether. I was trying to send a rather important contract to our legal department on Tuesday, and the entire server crashed. Just went completely black. I had to call the head of IT, Greg, at 9 PM. And you know what he said? He said they were running a routine update. A routine update! During the end-of-month closing period! I told him that if he didn't have the system back up in ten minutes, I'd outsource his entire department to a firm in Bangalore. I mean, honestly, the lack of foresight is staggering. You don't update servers when people are trying to finalize contracts. It's just common sense. \n\nBut yes, I have been snapping at people. Just... losing my temper over things that shouldn't matter.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Down? Depressed? No, I wouldn't say that. I don't sit around crying all day. My sister-in-law, Susan, was depressed after her divorce a few years ago, and she literally didn't get out of bed for a month. She just lay there watching daytime television. Have you actually seen what's on network TV in the middle of the day? It is utterly appalling. I had a mild bout of the flu last year and tried to watch some of those talk shows while I was resting, and it's just people screaming at each other about absolute nonsense. \n\nWho is the target audience for that? The advertisers must be throwing their money into a black hole. If my marketing director, David, ever suggested buying ad space during one of those programs, I'd have him clear out his desk by noon. We target a much more sophisticated demographic, obviously. We just launched a new print campaign in Architectural Digest, actually. The spread looks fantastic, though the publisher initially tried to put us on a left-hand page, and I absolutely refused. You always want the right-hand page, it's the first thing the eye goes to when you flip the magazine open. It took three phone calls and threatening to pull our funding entirely to sort it out. \n\nBut anyway, no, I'm not depressed like Susan was. I'm still going into the office every day and getting things done. I just... I don't feel much of anything at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like your book club, pilates, or even work accomplishments, no longer give you any sense of pleasure or satisfaction?\n\nPatient: Well, pleasure is a strong word, isn't it? And I behaved properly, of course, I said all the right things, but I just felt absolutely no warmth or relief inside. It was almost as if the affectionate part of me had just... gone completely quiet. \n\nWhich is strange, because I usually adore my nieces. They invested a huge chunk in this tech startup that was supposed to revolutionize online learning, and the whole thing folded in six months. I told them at the quarterly meeting last year that they needed to diversify into more stable blue-chip stocks, especially given the current market volatility. But do they listen? Of course not. They get these young hotshot financial advisors who think they can beat the market with some algorithm, and it always ends in tears. I mean, my own portfolio manager tried to push me into cryptocurrency last year, and I had to sit him down and explain exactly why that was a terrible idea for someone my age. \n\nBut anyway, yes, even with Sarah's good news, and the family gatherings, I just... I don't feel that spark of happiness I normally would. It's just flat.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt persistently sad, irritable, or emotionally down most of the day, nearly every day, since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said before, I wouldn't say I'm sad or depressed. I'm definitely more irritable, yes, snapping at my team and all that. But it's not this persistent sadness most of the day. It's more just... this flatness. \n\nSpeaking of flat, did you know the city is finally repaving the street in front of my office building? It's about time. The potholes were getting so bad, I thought I was going to ruin the suspension on my car. I drive a Mercedes S-Class, and let me tell you, the repair costs on those things are astronomical. I had a tiny scratch on the bumper last year, just from someone brushing past it in a parking garage, and the dealership wanted three thousand dollars to fix it. Three thousand! I told the service manager he was out of his mind and took it to an independent shop my brother-in-law recommended. They did it for eight hundred, and you can't even tell it was ever scratched. \n\nIt just goes to show you have to be so careful who you trust with your business these days. Everyone is looking for an opportunity to overcharge you. Just last week, our catering vendor for the executive board meetings tried to sneak in a twenty percent price increase on their invoices, hoping my assistant wouldn't notice. But she catches everything, thank goodness. I had her call them immediately and threaten to cancel our contract. They backed down right away, of course. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, right, being sad or down. No, I wouldn't say I feel sad most of the day. Just irritable and... empty, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even work accomplishments, which you used to take pride in, no longer feel satisfying or meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: Work accomplishments? Well, yes, I suppose. Though, honestly, the last time we went there, the service was abysmal. Our waiter kept disappearing, and it took forty-five minutes just to get our appetizers. I spoke to the manager, naturally, because if you're charging fifty dollars for a steak, the service should be impeccable. He tried to offer us a free dessert as an apology, which is such a lazy way to handle poor service. My nieces were there, trying so hard to comfort me, and I was going through the motions, behaving properly, but... there was just no warmth or relief inside. It really does feel like the affectionate, satisfied part of me has just gone completely quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad, frustrated, or on edge most of the time, even when there’s no specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, I wouldn't say I'm sad. But on edge? Frustrated? Yes, absolutely. I've been snapping at my leadership team over the most ridiculous things. Just yesterday, I laid into my VP of Operations over a tiny delay in a report and some wording in an email that I didn't like. It's like I mentioned before about the prayer gatherings for my cousin. My nieces were there, trying so hard to comfort me, and I was going through the motions, behaving properly, but... there was just no warmth or relief inside. It really does feel like the affectionate, satisfied part of me has just gone completely quiet.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt sad, frustrated, or on edge most of the time, even when there’s no specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, I wouldn't say I'm sad. But on edge? Frustrated? Yes, absolutely. I've been snapping at my leadership team over the most ridiculous things. Just yesterday, I laid into my VP of Operations over a tiny delay in a report and some wording in an email that I didn't like. I used a tone I would normally consider completely unacceptable, and then, of course, I felt terribly embarrassed afterward because they hadn't really done anything wrong. It's just... I have no patience right now. \n\nSpeaking of emails, the new IT system they rolled out last month is an absolute disaster. They promised us a seamless migration to this cloud-based platform, and it's been nothing but headaches. Half my inbox disappeared for three days, and when I called the help desk, I got routed to some call center where the person reading off a script couldn't even understand my problem. I finally had to threaten to pull the entire company's contract with the software provider before they assigned a dedicated technician to fix it. And even then, he had the nerve to suggest it might be user error! User error! I've been using email since before he was born. It's just like that time I tried to upgrade my home internet package and the cable company sent three different technicians out over two weeks, and none of them brought the right router. You just can't get competent help anymore, no matter how much you pay. \n\nBut yes, I am definitely more on edge and irritable, and taking it out on my team when they don't deserve it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even things you used to really enjoy — like your book club, pilates, or even work — feel completely flat or meaningless lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly. Like I was saying earlier about the prayer gatherings for my cousin. I was there, and my nieces were trying so hard to comfort me, you know, being very sweet. And I was behaving properly, doing what was expected, but... I just felt no warmth. No relief inside at all. It's almost as if the affectionate part of me has just gone completely quiet. \n\nIt’s funny you mention Pilates, though, because I actually canceled my membership last week. The new instructor they brought in—well, she’s just entirely too focused on breathing exercises instead of actual core work. I’m paying for a workout, not a meditation retreat. I told the studio manager that if I wanted to sit around and breathe, I’d go to a yoga studio. And don't even get me started on the parking situation there. They share a lot with a trendy brunch place, and on weekends, it is impossible to find a spot. I ended up having to park three blocks away in the rain once, wearing my good athletic shoes. I mean, the sheer lack of logistical planning for a high-end fitness studio is baffling. You'd think they would validate parking in the garage next door, but no, they claim it's \"too expensive\" for them to subsidize. \n\nBut anyway, to answer your question... yes. Everything just feels very flat right now. Even when I know I should be feeling something positive, it's just... quiet."
